Last weekend, the game's most devout [Legacy](/en/articles/event-coverage/2013/11-02-legacy-coverage-of-eternal-championships-2013) and [Vintage](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://magic.wizards.com/en/events/coverage/lims-fish-reel-deal-philly) players descended on Philadelphia, Pennsylvania, to battle it out at Eternal Weekend. Eternal formats are, at times, absurdly complex. Variance exists in sheer power, but the slower games are a place where the greatest players have an opportunity to maneuver themselves perfectly, playing around an opponent's interactive cards while extracting maximum value from each [Brainstorm](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Brainstorm) or [Wasteland](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Wasteland). Legacy had garnished a significant following over the last few years, and the **Magic Online** Legacy community is at its strongest; Legacy Daily Events not during graveyard hours have been firing consistently for the last few weeks. Today, we'll be taking a look at Legacy's best offerings from Eternal Weekend and **Magic Online**. Be ready to read a lot of new (very old) cards, because some of this stuff can get pretty wacky! 

Decks that combine [Sensei's Divining Top](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Sensei%27s+Divining+Top) with [Counterbalance](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Counterbalance) and [Entreat the Angels](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Entreat+the+Angels) are enjoying success both on **Magic Online** and in high-level face-to-face competition. Blue-Red-Green [Delver of Secrets](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Delver+of+Secrets) strategies and their variants are another huge portion of the metagame. Many other strategies that were believed to be dominant forces in the Legacy metagame were proven or disproved in the flurry of action from last weekend. Let's jump right into some decklists!

Blue-Red-Green [Delver of Secrets](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Delver+of+Secrets) tempo decks have been a dominant force in the Legacy metagame for a long time, but recent tournaments led many to believe that the deck had fallen from grace. The deck uses efficiently costed creatures and the game's best disruption/reach to close games quickly and decisively. Fortunately, the deck rose from the ashes last weekend and delivered tons of success at the Legacy Championships and 4–0ed plenty of Dailies on **Magic Online**. I chose to look at Brad Jarman's list because the numbers feel very right to me. Most RUG [Delver of Secrets](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/http://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?&name=Delver%2Bof%2BSecrets) lists are very similar these days, but a few copies of [Spell Pierce](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Spell+Pierce) and a singleton [Fire & Ice](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Fire+%26+Ice) make this deck capable of some very aggressive/disruptive games.

The first Dailies deck we'll be looking at today is an Esper [Stoneforge Mystic](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Stoneforge+Mystic) deck that uses some of the most powerful spells in the game to out-tempo its opponent in much the same way that a RUG [Delver of Secrets](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/http://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?&name=Delver%2Bof%2BSecrets) deck would. Playing these colors instead of red and green pushes the deck in a different direction, though. Instead of playing [Lightning Bolt](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Lightning+Bolt) effects, [Tarmogoyf](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Tarmogoyf), and [Nimble Mongoose](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Nimble+Mongoose), this deck uses [Geist of Saint Traft](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Geist+of+Saint+Traft), [Hymn to Tourach](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Hymn+to+Tourach), [Swords to Plowshares](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Swords+to+Plowshares), [Dark Confidant](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Dark+Confidant), and [Stoneforge Mystic](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Stoneforge+Mystic). The general idea of the deck is similar, but this deck doesn't push its best draws to the same limits as the RUG strategy, meaning that Esper Stoneforge players won't be receiving as many "free" wins. However, this deck's longevity and card-advantage engines mean that it's happy to go into a long game against almost every opponent.

Thraben";s:22:"field\_card\_image\_front";a:0:{}}s:4:"type";s:8:"creature";s:10:"deck\_count";i:0;}} 

  

Goblins may seem like it's outdated at this point, but the deck is very consistent, it grinds out card advantage better than almost every other strategy in the format, and it's nearly impossible to lose to opposing creature strategies. I like this list a lot in particular because of [Thalia, Guardian of Thraben](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Thalia%2C+Guardian+of+Thraben). Splashing white in the Goblin deck may seem like an odd way to get a big [Price of Progress](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Price+of+Progress) to the face or [Back to Basics](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Back+to+Basics) locking you down, but the defensive power offered by [Thalia, Guardian of Thraben](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Thalia%2C+Guardian+of+Thraben) against the combo strategies is unmatched. It's unlikely a combo opponent will sideboard to deal with [Thalia, Guardian of Thraben](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Thalia%2C+Guardian+of+Thraben) against Goblins, but players should be wary of this possibility going forward because it seems like exactly what the Goblin deck wants to do with its sideboard.



  

Here we see two different versions of the Miracles deck. The Miracles deck uses [Sensei's Divining Top](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Sensei%27s+Divining+Top) and [Brainstorm](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Brainstorm) to set up the top of its library in an effort to guarantee getting the miracle on [Entreat the Angels](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Entreat+the+Angels) or [Terminus](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Terminus), or to counter a spell for no cards with the help of [Counterbalance](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Counterbalance). Eventually, the Miracles player will have his or her opponent locked out of playing cheap spells and can clean up anything larger with full access to its massive range of reactive spells. 

<table><tbody><tr><td>&#10;						<div><div><img alt="Sensei's Divining Top" src="http://gatherer.wizards.com/Handlers/Image.ashx?type=card&amp;name=Sensei%27s+Divining+Top"></div></div>&#10;					</td>&#10;					<td>&#10;						<div><div><img alt="Entreat the Angels" src="http://gatherer.wizards.com/Handlers/Image.ashx?type=card&amp;name=Entreat+the+Angels"></div></div>&#10;					</td>&#10;				</tr></tbody></table>

The deck is strong against Combo and creature-based Aggro strategies, but it struggles with control mirrors and decks that use high casting costs primarily, like MUD. Both of these players chose different routes with their list, but the archetype has proven itself to be the clear front-runner for best Control strategy in Legacy. The [Enlightened Tutor](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Enlightened+Tutor) list has a much higher likelihood of playing a card that completely colds its opponent and locks up the game quickly, but the more stock version of the deck can reliably draw individually powerful cards that may be stronger in Control mirrors. True Control mirrors will be significantly less common in face-to-face tournaments, where playing a Legacy Control deck can be scary and difficult to finish rounds with. 

<table><tbody><tr><td>&#10;						<div><div><img alt="3489" src="http://gatherer.wizards.com/Handlers/Image.ashx?type=card&amp;multiverseid=3489"></div></div>&#10;					</td>&#10;					<td>&#10;						<div><div><img alt="Terminus" src="http://gatherer.wizards.com/Handlers/Image.ashx?type=card&amp;name=Terminus"></div></div>&#10;					</td>&#10;				</tr></tbody></table>

[Ad Nauseam](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Ad+Nauseam)[Tendrils](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Tendrils), or ANT, is one of the most explosive strategies in Legacy. The deck uses cards like [Dark Ritual](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Dark+Ritual) to produce a lot of mana and a high storm count in the early turns and kill the opponent with one massive [Tendrils of Agony](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Tendrils+of+Agony) after drawing a huge wad of cards with [Ad Nauseam](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Ad+Nauseam). The deck performs well on **Magic Online**, but its weakness to [Trinisphere](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Trinisphere); [Thalia, Guardian of Thraben](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Thalia%2C+Guardian+of+Thraben); and [Counterbalance](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Counterbalance) make it a liability at a face-to-face tournament, where the metagame tends to be much more diverse.

Legacy MUD is one of the more interesting decks in Legacy. The deck uses [Kuldotha Forgemaster](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Kuldotha+Forgemaster) to power out a lethal [Blightsteel Colossus](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Blightsteel+Colossus) or get its opponent down to no permanents with [Spine of Ish Sah](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Spine+of+Ish+Sah), while [Trinisphere](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Trinisphere) prevents the opponent from interacting in any way that could be relevant. An elusive creature, the deck occasionally emerges from a hole somewhere in the Midwest and crushes a tournament, but it's rare we see the deck do anything on **Magic Online**, mostly because such a small sample of people have put it together.

One of the biggest questions coming into Eternal Weekend was whether or not we would see [True-Name Nemesis](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=True-Name+Nemesis) at the top tables. From what I heard, only a handful of Wise-Williams chose to play the card, and Pro Tour Champion Osyp Lebedowicz was able to pilot his Blue-Red [Delver of Secrets](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/http://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?&name=Delver%2Bof%2BSecrets) deck all the way to the finals of the tournament. The Blue-Red [Delver of Secrets](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/http://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?&name=Delver%2Bof%2BSecrets) deck is more consistent than its green counterpart, and [True-Name Nemesis](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=True-Name+Nemesis) is surely going to be one of the better cards for a tempo strategy in Legacy. Sure, cards like [Terminus](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Terminus), [Engineered Explosives](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Engineered+Explosives), and [Wrath of God](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Wrath+of+God) exist, but an opponent will usually be spending more mana or resources removing a [True-Name Nemesis](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=True-Name+Nemesis) that already cracked him or her in the head than it cost to play the [True-Name Nemesis](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=True-Name+Nemesis) in the first place. I like Osyp's list a lot; [Young Pyromancer](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Young+Pyromancer) can make for some very flooded boards on the earliest turns of the game and the deck gets access to [Blood Moon](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Blood+Moon) for post-sideboarded games, allowing it to pick up free wins even when it doesn't get to protect a turn-one [Delver of Secrets](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Delver+of+Secrets) or turn-three [True-Name Nemesis](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=True-Name+Nemesis).

The only two things that are truly inevitable are Death and Taxes. The deck aims to beat its opponent down with an array of hard-to-deal-with creatures that disrupt the opponent. [Mangara of Corondor](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Mangara+of+Corondor) may seem like an odd inclusion, but one can use [Mangara of Corondor](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Mangara+of+Corondor) in conjunction with [Æther Vial](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=%C3%86ther+Vial) set on three and [Karakas](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Karakas) to get a one-mana "exile target permanent" every turn of the game. 

<table><tbody><tr><td>&#10;						<div><div><img alt="Mangara of Corondor" src="http://gatherer.wizards.com/Handlers/Image.ashx?type=card&amp;name=Mangara+of+Corondor"></div></div>&#10;					</td>&#10;					<td>&#10;						<div><div><img alt="Karakas" src="http://gatherer.wizards.com/Handlers/Image.ashx?type=card&amp;name=Karakas"></div></div>&#10;					</td>&#10;				</tr></tbody></table>

Ari Lax proved the deck's strength (and his own mastery) this weekend with this incredibly tuned White Weenie deck. The deck has a ton of play no matter how you look at it, and its general baseline of cards, while not as impressive in terms of sheer power, are well suited to deal with the current Legacy metagame. One thing that particularly impresses me is Lax's sideboard. Two copies of [Enlightened Tutor](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Enlightened+Tutor) alongside one or two copies of a number of HIGH IMPACT/inexpensive artifacts and enchantments mean that Lax essentially gets to play three or four copies of nine different cards, in addition to a miser's copy of [Sunlance](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Sunlance) and [Mindbreak Trap](https://web.archive.org/web/20131107000000/https://gatherer.wizards.com/Pages/Card/Details.aspx?name=Mindbreak+Trap) for the appropriate matchups. 

<table><tbody><tr><td>&#10;						<div><div><img alt="Sunlance" src="http://gatherer.wizards.com/Handlers/Image.ashx?type=card&amp;name=Sunlance"></div></div>&#10;					</td>&#10;					<td>&#10;						<div><div><img alt="Mindbreak Trap" src="http://gatherer.wizards.com/Handlers/Image.ashx?type=card&amp;name=Mindbreak+Trap"></div></div>&#10;					</td>&#10;				</tr></tbody></table>

Legacy continues to evolve despite the pool of cards being over twenty years deep. [*Vintage Masters*](/en/articles/feature/2013/10-21-introducing-vintage-masters) looms on the **Magic Online** horizon and I suspect the availability of those cards on **Magic Online** will push competitive Vintage to a new level in the coming months. Remember, the player has a lot more to do with winning than the deck when you play Legacy. Practice hard, be honest with yourself and numbers, and take as much criticism from well-versed Legacy players as you can. 

Knowledge is Power!
